- DoDD 5205.07, DoD Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (12 Sep 2024) runs out to **Acknowledged, unacknowledged and waived, the three states of a DoD SAP** (domain, board tier A), a denial link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The 2024 reissue deletes acknowledged, unacknowledged, waived and carve-out from the definitions. The glossary of the 12 September 2024 directive substitutes 'Joint Force Integration SAP' and 'Strategic Enabler SAP' and contains none of the four prior terms. Sources: DoD Directive 5205.07, 12 September 2024, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2024-09-12.
- DoDD 5205.07, DoD Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (12 Sep 2024) runs out to **DoDD 5205.07, Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (1 Jul 2010, Chg 2 of 4 Feb 2020)** (document, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: 12 Sep 2024 reissuance cancels the 1 Jul 2010 directive. Same policy instrument, two issuances, and the programmatic relation is what CHANGED between them - which is not the same fact as the supersession recorded on the temporal edge. The 2010 directive's glossary defines 'carve-out' as 'a provision approved by the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense that relieves DSS of its National Industrial Security Program oversight', and its Enclosure 2 para 5 restates the statutory waiver in policy. A full-text pass of the 12 September 2024 reissue finds no occurrence of 'carve-out', 'waived' or 'unacknowledged' at all. The vocabulary the earlier directive defined is simply absent from the later one. Note what that does and does not establish: 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) still carries the carve-out in codified regulation, and still points at DoDD 5205.07 by name, so the mechanism did not end when the words left the directive. Sources: Archived: DoD Directive 5205.07, 'DoD Special Access Program (SAP) Policy', effective 12 September 2024 (Wayback 2026-05-18), https://web.archive.org/web/20260518065512/https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2026-05-18; DoD Directive 5205.07, 'DoD Special Access Program (SAP) Policy', effective 12 September 2024, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2024-09-12.
- DoDD 5205.07, DoD Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (12 Sep 2024) runs out to **DoDD 5205.07, Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (1 Jul 2010, Chg 2 of 4 Feb 2020)** (document, board tier A), a temporal link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Cancelled and replaced the 2010 directive on 12 September 2024. The 2024 reissue cancels DoDD 5205.07 of 1 July 2010 as amended and incorporates the 11 July 2023 Deputy Secretary memorandum on SAP Enterprise Reform. Sources: DoD Directive 5205.07, 12 September 2024, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2024-09-12.
- DoDD 5205.07, DoD Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (12 Sep 2024) comes in from **DoD special access programme governance change, 5 January 1994** (event, board tier B), a temporal link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: The later directive re-established a body of the same name. The 1994 bulletin and the 1994 commission report both describe a SAPOC chaired by the Deputy Secretary of Defense with an annual validation function, which is the function the later directive and instruction assign. Sources: SAPOC Information Bulletin, November 1994, read against the dodd-5205-07-2024 node, https://sgp.fas.org/othergov/sapoc.html, dated 1994-11-01.
- DoDD 5205.07, DoD Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (12 Sep 2024) comes in from **Carve-Out Provision (DCSA relief from NISP oversight)** (program, board tier A), a denial link at Tier 3 stated inference. The link reads: Still cited by regulation, no longer defined by the directive it cites. 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) conditions the carve-out on approval 'in accordance with DoDD 5205.07', but the 12 September 2024 reissue of that directive contains no definition of carve-out. Sources: 32 CFR 117.6 (eCFR, current), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.6, dated 2026-08-12; DoD Directive 5205.07, 12 September 2024, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2024-09-12.

## DoD OIG - Evaluation of the DoD's Actions Regarding Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena (DODIG-2023-109)

DoD OIG - Evaluation of the DoD's Actions Regarding Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena (DODIG-2023-109) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2023 to 2024; axes C1 A3 D3; 5 connections.

In one line: Classified report 15 Aug 2023; unclassified summary released 25 Jan 2024.

### What it is

The Inspector General's evaluation of how the Department handles UAP, completed 15 August 2023 and released in unclassified summary on 25 January 2024. The full report is classified; the public has the summary only. Its findings are structural and damning in an undramatic way: the Department has no overarching UAP policy; different DoD components each developed their own separate processes for collecting, analysing and identifying UAP incidents; and as a result the Department cannot ensure that national security and flight safety threats from UAP have been identified and addressed. It made eleven recommendations to the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security, the service secretaries and the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs, including development of a department-wide policy to integrate UAP roles, responsibilities, requirements and coordination procedures into existing intelligence, counterintelligence and force protection policies. For this board the finding that matters is the fragmentation one. An office that formally reviews only what it can reach, inside a department the IG says has no unified UAP process and component-level stovepipes, cannot produce a negative finding of departmental scope - and every AARO negative finding since has been phrased as 'AARO has discovered no evidence', which is a statement about AARO's reach, not the Department's contents. The classification of the full report means the IG's own account of where the stovepipes are remains unavailable, including through PURSUE.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 72 of 1082, score 69). 6 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Consequence finding: 'We determined that the DoD has no overarching UAP policy and, as a result, it lacks assurance that national security and flight safety threats to the United States from UAP have been identified and mitigated.' Source: https://media.defense.gov/2024/Jan/25/2003381266/-1/-1/1/UNCLASSIFIED%20SUMMARY_UNIDENTIFIED%20ANOMALOUS%20PHENOMENA%20SECURE.PDF
- **Tier 1 documented.** DODIG-2023-109, dated 15 August 2023, evaluated how the DoD, military services, defense agencies and counterintelligence organizations detect, report, collect, analyze and identify UAP; the unclassified summary was released 25 January 2024. Source: DoD OIG report page and press release
- **Tier 1 documented.** On the absence of a response plan: 'The DoD has not issued a comprehensive UAP response plan that identifies roles, responsibilities, requirements, and coordination procedures... As a result, the DoD response to UAP incidents is uncoordinated and concentrated within each Military Department. Officials from the Military Services and MDCOs stated that they have been waiting for the DoD to issue comprehensive UAP guidance before developing their own guidance.' Source: https://media.defense.gov/2024/Jan/25/2003381266/-1/-1/1/UNCLASSIFIED%20SUMMARY_UNIDENTIFIED%20ANOMALOUS%20PHENOMENA%20SECURE.PDF
- **Tier 1 documented.** Recommendation 1: 'We recommend that the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security, in coordination with the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office Director, issue a Department of Defense policy to integrate unidentified anomalous phenomena roles, responsibilities, requirements, and coordination procedures into existing intelligence, counterintelligence, and force protection policies and procedures. The policy should include methods to address unidentified anomalous phenomena incidents and should align with policies and procedures for the protection of United States persons' civil liberties.' USD(I&S) and the AARO Director agreed; the recommendation was left 'resolved but open'. Source: https://media.defense.gov/2024/Jan/25/2003381266/-1/-1/1/UNCLASSIFIED%20SUMMARY_UNIDENTIFIED%20ANOMALOUS%20PHENOMENA%20SECURE.PDF
- **Tier 1 documented.** The DoD IG found the Department lacks a comprehensive, coordinated approach to UAP and had not issued a comprehensive UAP response plan. Source: DODIG-2023-109 unclassified summary, January 2024
- **Tier 1 documented.** The IG found DoD does not formally require components to report UAP incidents to AARO, and that reporting systems differ across services including the Air Force's Marauder system. Source: DODIG-2023-109 unclassified summary
- **Tier 1 documented.** The IG found geographic combatant commanders were largely excluded from UAP policy development despite bearing statutory responsibility for threat detection. Source: DODIG-2023-109 unclassified summary
- **Tier 1 documented.** The IG found that DoD lacks an overarching UAP policy, that components developed separate processes, and that the Department cannot ensure national security and flight safety threats from UAP have been identified and addressed. Source: DoD OIG press release on DODIG-2023-109

A further 8 claims sit on this node on the board and are not reproduced here.

## DoDM 5205.07, DoD SAP Security Manual (Volumes 1-4)

DoDM 5205.07, DoD SAP Security Manual (Volumes 1-4) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2012-01-01; axes C1 A3 D1; 5 connections.

In one line: dod-issuance.

### What it is

The DoD SAP Security Manual is issued in four volumes under number 5205.07: Volume 1, General Procedures (18 June 2015, Change 1 12 February 2018); Volume 2, Personnel Security; Volume 3, Physical Security; and Volume 4, Marking. Volume 1 is the volume that carries the carve-out definition and the DSS relief provision, and is the only volume this board has been able to obtain in full from an archived copy of the DoD Issuances site; the current live URLs at esd.whs.mil for volumes 1 through 4 return errors to unauthenticated retrieval as of 12 August 2026, which is itself a finding worth recording. Volume 1 states its own lineage: it 'Assigns responsibilities, implements policy established in DoD Instruction (DoDI) 5205.11 ... and describes the general procedures for the administration of DoD SAP security' and 'Incorporates and cancels Revision 1 Department of Defense Overprint to the National Industrial Security Program (NISP) Operating Manual Supplement'. That last clause is the historical bridge: the security rules for contractor SAPs used to live as an overprint to the NISPOM Supplement, i.e. as a special-case annex to the industrial security system, and were pulled out into a standalone DoD manual - moving contractor SAP security further from the NISP baseline that DCSA inspects.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 60 of 1082, score 73). 5 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D1.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDM 5205.07 Vol 1 glossary: 'carve-out. A provision approved by the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense that relieves DSS of its NISP obligation to perform industrial security oversight functions for a DoD SAP.' Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDM 5205.07 Vol 2, para 5 (Polygraphs): 'The Deputy Secretary of Defense is the approval authority for the use of polygraph examination as a mandatory access determination requirement; the requirement must be consistently applied to all candidates in accordance with DoDI 5210.91. CI-Scope polygraph examination must not be used as the only basis for granting access to DoD SAPs. Exceptions to these requirements will only be granted by the Deputy Secretary of Defense. ... CI polygraph examinations are considered current when administered within the past 5 years.' Para 6 provides that 'CA SAPCOs may establish or authorize SAP billet structures or access quotas that assign individual access by organization and duty position to SAPs under their cognizance.' Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20250305140253/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol2.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1 applies to all DoD Components, to 'All DoD Component contractors and consultants who require access to DoD SAPs pursuant to the terms and conditions of the contract or agreement', and to non-DoD USG entities requiring access under an MOA. Source: DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1, para 2
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1 states that it 'Incorporates and cancels Revision 1 Department of Defense Overprint to the National Industrial Security Program (NISP) Operating Manual Supplement.' Source: DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1, para 1.a(2)
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE CARVE-OUT'S OPERATIONAL CONTENT. DoDM 5205.07 Vol 1, Enclosure 2, para 2 (Director, DSS): 'Unless a carve-out provision is approved by the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense: (1) Performs SAP security inspections at cleared defense contractor locations in accordance with this volume, Reference (f), and the government contracting activity's completed DD Form 254 ... (2) Authorizes classified contractor SAP information systems (ISs) and the introduction of guest systems in contractor SAP facilities (SAPFs). (3) Coordinates with the designated counterintelligence (CI) component to provide cross-sharing of threat and incident information affecting the security of the facility or its defense information or cleared personnel.' A carve-out removes all three functions at once: physical inspection, IT accreditation, and CI information sharing. Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Vol 1 physical-security/visitor rules acknowledge the existence of undisclosed sites in operative text: 'Federal Government-affiliated identification cards will not be used for positive identification in unacknowledged locations.' Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Vol 1 states that inspections 'will be coordinated among the SAPCOs and DSS when not carved out and conducted jointly to the greatest extent possible' -- confirming that when a SAP IS carved out, the only remaining inspection is the program's own self-inspection plus SAPCO-internal compliance inspection. Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Vol 1, Enclosure 4, para 4: 'The CUA documents areas of authorities and responsibilities between cognizant security offices (CSOs) when they share the same SAPF. ... The first CSO in an area, unless otherwise agreed upon, will be considered the host CSO responsible for all security oversight.' And, critically: 'For CUAs, the responsible organization will be identified for executing security cognizance with a carved-out SAP.' Multiple SAPs -- including carved-out ones -- can lawfully co-occupy one accredited facility under a written host/tenant agreement. Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf

A further 6 claims sit on this node on the board and are not reproduced here.

### Its strongest connections

- DoDM 5205.07, DoD SAP Security Manual (Volumes 1-4) runs out to **DoD Special Access Program Central Office (DoD SAPCO)** (institution, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: assigns it the patent and inspection roles. Volume 1 Enclosure 4 para 7: 'PATENTS AND INTELLECTUAL PROPERTY. The CA SAPCO will develop procedures for processing patents and intellectual property involving SAP(s).' Volume 1 also routes inspections through the SAPCOs jointly with DSS where a programme is not carved out. Both are verbatim from this node's recorded quotations, and the patents provision is the one that matters most to this board: it is an explicit channel for intellectual property arising inside a special access programme. The link reads: manual implements policy established in DoDD 5205.07. The Manual implements the Directive. The 2010 directive sets SAP policy and defines the carve-out in its glossary; the four-volume Manual carries the operating detail - Volume 1's Enclosure 2 on DSS oversight and its relief, Enclosure 4 on whistleblower routing through SAP channels, and the patents and intellectual property provision. Directive states the policy, Manual runs it. DoDM 5205.07 is issued in volumes; Volume 1 carries the responsibilities, general procedures, HVSACO rules and the glossary containing the carve-out definition. ## DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1, DoD SAP Security Manual: General Procedures

DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1, DoD SAP Security Manual: General Procedures is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2015-06-18; axes C1 A3 D2; 5 connections.

In one line: dod-issuance.

### What it is

Volume 1 of the four-volume DoD SAP Security Manual assigns responsibilities and describes the general procedures for the administration of DoD SAP security, implementing the policy of DoDI 5205.11, and it incorporates and cancels the Revision 1 Department of Defense Overprint to the National Industrial Security Program Operating Manual Supplement. Its Enclosure 2 contains the operative statement of what a carve-out removes. The Director, DSS - the office now called the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency - administers the NISP and issues facility clearances for defense contractors 'to include contractors performing on DoD SAPs'; but then, 'Unless a carve-out provision is approved by the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense', the Director performs SAP security inspections at cleared defense contractor locations, authorises classified contractor SAP information systems and the introduction of guest systems in contractor SAP facilities, and coordinates with the designated counterintelligence component to cross-share threat and incident information. Read the sentence backwards and it is an inventory of what disappears when a carve-out is signed: the independent physical inspection, the independent accreditation of the computers, and the independent counterintelligence feed. Its glossary supplies the definition in eleven words of substance: 'carve-out. A provision approved by the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense that relieves DSS of its NISP obligation to perform industrial security oversight functions for a DoD SAP.' Volume 1 also documents two cover mechanisms - HANDLE VIA SPECIAL ACCESS CHANNELS ONLY (HVSACO), an unclassified handling caveat used among other things 'When using a SAP nickname for an unacknowledged SAP', and the rule that 'Federal Government-affiliated identification cards will not be used for positive identification in unacknowledged locations'.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 105 of 1082, score 60). 5 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A3/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDM 5205.07-V1 Glossary: 'carve-out. A provision approved by the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense that relieves DSS of its NISP obligation to perform industrial security oversight functions for a DoD SAP.' Source: DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1, Glossary Part II

Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf

## Estimate of the Situation (1948)

Estimate of the Situation (1948) is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1948; axes C1 A3 D2; 5 connections.

In one line: Top Secret Project SIGN assessment; destroyed.

### What it is

In mid-1948 the Project SIGN staff at Wright Field completed a Top Secret document, in the standard military 'estimate of the situation' format, concluding that the objects under investigation were interplanetary in origin. It went up the chain to Air Force Chief of Staff Gen. Hoyt Vandenberg, who rejected it on the grounds that it lacked proof and directed that copies be destroyed. No copy has ever surfaced. This board treats the Estimate as the single most consequential missing document of the twentieth century on this subject, and its status is stronger than folklore: the Condon Report, commissioned and paid for by the Air Force under contract 44620-67-C-0035, states in Section V Chapter 2 that the estimate was prepared, that Vandenberg rejected it, and that copies were destroyed. Edward Ruppelt, the first Blue Book director, published the same account in 1956 from personal knowledge. Against this, Air Force spokesmen and members of Congress have at times asserted no such estimate ever existed. The conflict is itself the datum: the government's own contracted history records a document the government elsewhere denies. It also establishes, twenty years before the Bolender memo and forty-seven before the GAO records finding, that destruction was the standard disposition for adverse conclusions.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 308 of 1082, score 37). 5 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A3/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 'Staff members prepared a classified Estimate of the Situation in July 1948 asserting UFOs were interplanetary vehicles. Air Force Chief of Staff Gen. Vandenberg rejected it for lacking proof, and copies were destroyed.' Source: Condon Report, Section V Chapter 2, 'UFOs 1947-1968' (University of Colorado, USAF contract 44620-67-C-0035, 1968)
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Air Force representatives and Rep. L. Mendel Rivers have asserted that no such Estimate ever existed. Source: Congressional and Air Force statements catalogued in the secondary literature on Project SIGN
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Ruppelt first published the existence, content and destruction of the Estimate in 1956 based on his tenure as Blue Book director. Source: Ruppelt, 'The Report on Unidentified Flying Objects', 1956
- **Tier 3 stated inference.** A government-contracted official report and official denials cannot both be correct; the contracted report was written with access to Air Force files and the denials were made publicly, which favours the report. Source: Inference from the conflict between Condon Sec. V Ch. 2 and later official denials

### Its strongest connections

- Estimate of the Situation (1948) comes in from **University of Colorado UFO Project (Condon Committee)** (program, board tier A), a investigative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The Condon Report is the primary documentary record of the Estimate's destruction. Section V Chapter 2 records the estimate's preparation, Vandenberg's rejection and the destruction of copies, the Air Force paying to put its own suppression on the record. Sources: Condon Report Sec. V Ch. 2, https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/s5chap02.htm, dated 1968-11-01.
- Estimate of the Situation (1948) comes in from **Project SIGN** (program, board tier B), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: SIGN staff produced the 1948 Estimate. Project SIGN personnel wrote a Top Secret estimate concluding the objects were interplanetary. The Condon Report, written under Air Force contract, records that it was prepared. Sources: Condon Report Sec. V Ch. 2, https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/s5chap02.htm, dated 1968-11-01.
- Estimate of the Situation (1948) comes in from **Hoyt S. Vandenberg** (person, board tier B), a denial link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Vandenberg rejected the Estimate and ordered copies destroyed. The Air Force Chief of Staff rejected the interplanetary conclusion for lacking proof and had the document destroyed, the earliest documented destruction of an adverse UAP record, attested in the Air Force's own contracted study. Sources: Condon Report Sec. V Ch. 2, https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/s5chap02.htm, dated 1968-11-01; Ruppelt, The Report on Unidentified Flying Objects, https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17346, dated 1956-01-01.
- Estimate of the Situation (1948) runs out to **Project GRUDGE** (program, board tier C), a temporal link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Destruction of the Estimate preceded GRUDGE's dismissive posture by six months. SIGN became GRUDGE on 11 February 1949, months after the Estimate was destroyed, and GRUDGE's founding conclusions ran directly counter to it. Sources: Condon Report Sec. V Ch. 2, https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/s5chap02.htm, dated 1968-11-01.
- Estimate of the Situation (1948) comes in from **Edward J. Ruppelt** (person, board tier B), a witness-chain link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: Ruppelt is the origin of public knowledge of the Estimate. As a serving Blue Book director with access to the files, Ruppelt published the Estimate's existence and destruction in 1956; the Condon Report corroborated him twelve years later. Sources: Ruppelt, The Report on Unidentified Flying Objects, https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/17346, dated 1956-01-01; Condon Report Sec. V Ch. 2, https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/s5chap02.htm, dated 1968-11-01.

## Foreign Technology Division, Wright-Patterson AFB

Foreign Technology Division, Wright-Patterson AFB is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1961-07-01 to 1991; axes C2 A3 D1; 5 connections.

In one line: Air Force Systems Command foreign materiel exploitation centre, 1961-1991; the office that held Project Blue Book.

### What it is

The Foreign Technology Division was the Air Force's foreign aerospace materiel exploitation centre at Wright-Patterson AFB, sitting between the Air Technical Intelligence Center and the National Air and Space Intelligence Center in the same lineage and at the same station. Air Force Regulation 80-17 of 19 September 1966 assigned it the Blue Book effort in its own name: FTD analysed and evaluated every report a base could not identify, and wrote the final case report on each. The USAF Scientific Advisory Board's ad hoc review committee of March 1966 called it the cognizant Air Force agency for UFO sightings and recommended it keep the job, while recording that the whole programme ran on one officer, a sergeant and a secretary. The board carries this organisation in prose twenty-one times and has never had a node for it.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 240 of 1082, score 41). 5 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D1.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** FTD, not a base, was the terminal analytical office: 'FTD will: (1) Analyze and evaluate all information and evidence reported to bases on those UFOs which are not identified at the base level', and 'FTD, Wright-Patterson AFB, Ohio, will prepare a final case report on each sighting reported to it.' Source: Air Force Regulation 80-17, 19 September 1966, https://nicap.org/afr80-17.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Blue Book effort was assigned to FTD by regulation: 'The Air Force Systems Command will support the program with current resources within the Foreign Technology Division (FTD) at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio, to continue Blue Book effort.' Source: Air Force Regulation 80-17, 'Research and Development - UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS (UFO)', Department of the Air Force, 19 September 1966, https://nicap.org/afr80-17.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** The USAF Scientific Advisory Board's ad hoc committee of March 1966 identified FTD as 'the AFSC Foreign Technology Division, which is the cognizant Air Force agency that collates information on UFO sightings and monitors investigations of individual cases', and recommended that it 'should continue to exercise its presently assigned responsibilities concerning UFOs.' Source: Special Report of the USAF Scientific Advisory Board Ad Hoc Committee to Review Project 'Blue Book', March 1966, printed as Appendix A of the Condon Report, https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/appndx-a.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** The same 1966 committee recorded the resources behind the whole Air Force UFO programme as 'only one officer, a sergeant, and secretary'. Source: Special Report of the USAF Scientific Advisory Board Ad Hoc Committee to Review Project 'Blue Book', March 1966, Condon Report Appendix A, https://files.ncas.org/condon/text/appndx-a.htm
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The Air Force organizational-history lineage records a discontinuation and a new constitution rather than a renaming: 'Air Technical Intelligence Center established 21 May 1951; redesignated Aerospace Technical Intelligence Center, 21 September 1959; discontinued 1 July 1961. Foreign Technology Division constituted, activated, and organized 1 July 1961', with the mission and resources absorbed by FTD on that date. Tier 2 because the compilation carrying this text is a private site citing the Air Force Historical Research Agency, and AFHRA's own page returns HTTP 403 from this environment. Source: 'AEROSPACE TECHNICAL INTELLIGENCE CENTER', Department of the Air Force Organizational Histories compilation, created 10 December 2025, sourced to the Air Force Historical Research Agency, https://www.usafunithistory.com/PDF/A-E/AEROSPACE%20TECHNICAL%20INTELLIGENCE%20CENTER.pdf

### Its strongest connections

- Foreign Technology Division, Wright-Patterson AFB runs out to **Project Blue Book** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Held the Blue Book effort by regulation from 19 September 1966. AFR 80-17 assigns the Blue Book effort to FTD at Wright-Patterson, requires FTD to analyse every report not identified at base level, and requires FTD to write the final case report on each sighting. Sources: AFR 80-17, 19 September 1966, https://nicap.org/afr80-17.htm, dated 1966-09-19.
- Foreign Technology Division, Wright-Patterson AFB runs out to **United States Air Force** (institution, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Air Force Systems Command organisation under the DCS/Research and Development for the UFO programme. AFR 80-17 makes the Deputy Chief of Staff, Research and Development responsible for the overall programme and Air Force Systems Command responsible for supporting it through FTD. Sources: AFR 80-17, 19 September 1966, https://nicap.org/afr80-17.htm, dated 1966-09-19.
- Foreign Technology Division, Wright-Patterson AFB runs out to **Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio** (location, board tier A), a geographic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Stationed at Wright-Patterson AFB, Ohio. AFR 80-17 names the station in the assignment itself: 'the Foreign Technology Division (FTD) at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio'. Sources: AFR 80-17, 19 September 1966, https://nicap.org/afr80-17.htm, dated 1966-09-19.
- Foreign Technology Division, Wright-Patterson AFB comes in from **Air Technical Intelligence Center** (institution, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: ATIC discontinued 1 July 1961; FTD constituted the same day and absorbed its mission. The lineage record says ATIC was redesignated Aerospace Technical Intelligence Center in 1959 and discontinued on 1 July 1961, with the mission and resources absorbed by the newly constituted Foreign Technology Division, so this is a succession rather than the renaming the board's prose describes. Sources: Aerospace Technical Intelligence Center, DAF Organizational Histories compilation sourced to AFHRA, https://www.usafunithistory.com/PDF/A-E/AEROSPACE%20TECHNICAL%20INTELLIGENCE%20CENTER.pdf, dated 2025-12-10.
- Foreign Technology Division, Wright-Patterson AFB runs out to **National Air and Space Intelligence Center** (institution, board tier C), a institutional link at Tier 2 named person on the record. Sources: Aerospace Technical Intelligence Center, DAF Organizational Histories compilation sourced to AFHRA, https://www.usafunithistory.com/PDF/A-E/AEROSPACE%20TECHNICAL%20INTELLIGENCE%20CENTER.pdf, dated 2025-12-10.

## Death of Frank R. Olson, 1953, and the compensation and litigation that followed

Death of Frank R. Olson, 1953, and the compensation and litigation that followed is a event on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1953-11-19 to 2013-07-17; axes C0 A1 D3; 5 connections.

In one line: Army civilian dosed with LSD without his knowledge at a CIA meeting on 19 November 1953; died in New York days later; compensated by Private Law 94-126 in 1976; the family's 2012 suit dismissed in D.D.C. in 2013.

### What it is

This is the one death inside a compartmented American programme that the government conceded, compensated by statute, and then litigated over sixty years later, and it belongs on this board because the paper trail exists on both sides. Frank R. Olson, a civilian employee of the Army at Fort Detrick, was given LSD without his knowledge in a bottle of Cointreau at a CIA and Army meeting on 19 November 1953 and died in New York within about a week. The Rockefeller Commission disclosed the connection in June 1975 without naming him, and attached to its own account a footnote saying the few remaining Agency records indicate he may have had a history of emotional instability. Congress paid his widow and three children $187,500 each in 1976 in exchange for a full waiver, and President Ford signed it saying his administration had wanted twice that. In 2012 two of the children sued the United States alleging the death was a murder concealed for sixty years; the District of Columbia dismissed the suit in 2013 on timeliness, the 1976 waiver and sovereign immunity, while recording that the public record supports many of the allegations. Four opened records disagree about which floor he fell from, what day he died and whether anyone was reprimanded.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 198 of 1082, score 44). 5 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A1/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Congress compensated the family by private statute. Private Law 94-126, 90 Stat. 3006 (1976), directs payment of 'the sum of $187,500 each to Alice W. Olson, Lisa Olson Hayward, Eric Olson, and Nils Olson, in full settlement of all of their claims against the United States arising out of the death of Doctor Frank R. Olson in November 1953, if all of them waive any and all rights arising out of such death.' Source: Private Law 94-126, 90 Stat. 3006 (1976), quoted in full in Olson v. United States, No. 12-1924 (JEB), D.D.C., memorandum opinion of 17 July 2013, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCOURTS-dcd-1_12-cv-01924/pdf/USCOURTS-dcd-1_12-cv-01924-0.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** President Ford signed the bill on 12 October 1976 and stated the administration's own position on the amount: 'Legislation to appropriate $1,250,000 to compensate the wife and three children of Dr. Olson for his death was supported by this administration. However, the Congress approved a measure calling for the payment of a total of $750,000 to these survivors.' Source: Gerald R. Ford, Statement on Signing the Bill Providing for the Relief of the Survivors of Dr. Frank R. Olson, 12 October 1976, https://www.presidency.ucsb.edu/documents/statement-signing-the-bill-providing-for-the-relief-the-survivors-dr-frank-r-olson
- **Tier 1 documented, allegation shaped wording as stored.** The 2012 suit was brought in the District of Columbia, not Maryland, and was dismissed in 2013 without reaching the merits: 'Concluding that most of the allegations are both untimely and waived by a prior settlement agreement, and that any timely or preserved claims fall outside of the United States' waiver of sovereign immunity, the Court will grant the Government's Motion.' The same opinion records that 'the skeptical reader may wish to know that the public record supports many of the allegations that follow, farfetched as they may sound.' Source: Eric Olson and Nils Olson v. United States of America, Civil Action No. 12-1924 (JEB), United States District Court for the District of Columbia, memorandum opinion filed 17 July 2013, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCOURTS-dcd-1_12-cv-01924/pdf/USCOURTS-dcd-1_12-cv-01924-0.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Rockefeller Commission's June 1975 report disclosed the death without naming him: 'on one occasion during the early phases of this program (in 1953), LSD was administered to an employee of the Department of the Army without his knowledge ... Several days later, he jumped from a tenth floor window of his room and died as a result.' Its footnote to that passage reads: 'There are indications in the few remaining Agency records that this individual may have had a history of emotional instability.' Source: Report to the President by the Commission on CIA Activities within the United States, June 1975, chapter 16, pp. 227-228, https://documents2.theblackvault.com/documents/rockefellercommissionreport-full.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The four opened records do not agree. The Rockefeller Commission and President Ford give a tenth floor window; the Church Committee text in the 1977 Senate print gives a tenth story window of the Statler Hotel and the death as 27 November 1953; Ford and the 2012 complaint give 28 November; the complaint gives thirteen stories at 2:30 a.m. The CIA told the Senate in 1977 that he 'leaped to his death from a hotel room window in New York City about a week after having unwittingly consumed LSD'. Source: Project MKULTRA joint hearing, 3 August 1977 (Turner statement and Appendix B); Rockefeller Commission report, June 1975; Ford signing statement, 12 October 1976; Olson v. United States, D.D.C., 17 July 2013, https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/sites/default/files/hearings/95mkultra.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The same report records the ruling that produced death benefits and the discipline that followed: 'The General Counsel ruled that the death resulted from "circumstances arising out of an experiment undertaken in the course of his official duties for the United States Government," thus ensuring his survivors of receiving certain death benefits. Reprimands were issued by the Director of Central Intelligence to two CIA employees responsible for the incident.' The 2012 complaint states the opposite on the second point, that the internal investigation issued no formal reprimands. Source: Report to the President by the Commission on CIA Activities within the United States, June 1975, p. 227, https://documents2.theblackvault.com/documents/rockefellercommissionreport-full.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record, allegation shaped wording as stored.** On the Danny Jones Podcast, episode 100, 1 July 2021, at 2:10:37, Tom O'Neill said Eric Olson has spent his life arguing his father was thrown from a window at the 'Statler Hilton' in New York in 1953 rather than having jumped, and that the government's own position is that Olson was used in an experiment but died by suicide. The opened records name the Statler Hotel, and the murder allegation is the 2012 complaint's, dismissed in 2013 on timeliness, waiver and sovereign immunity rather than on its truth. Source: Danny Jones Podcast, ep. 100, 1 July 2021, 2:10:37 to 2:11:31

### Its strongest connections

- Death of Frank R. Olson, 1953, and the compensation and litigation that followed runs out to **Project MKULTRA** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The death that made the cryptonym public. DCI Turner told the Senate in 1977 that the cryptonym MKULTRA became publicly known during the Rockefeller Commission and Church Committee investigations of 1975 when details of the drug-related death of Dr. Frank Olson were publicized. ## Fleet Forces Command 'URGENT SAFETY OF FLIGHT ISSUE' Email (Jan 2015, recalled)

Fleet Forces Command 'URGENT SAFETY OF FLIGHT ISSUE' Email (Jan 2015, recalled) is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2015; axes C1 A3 D3; 5 connections.

In one line: Recalled Navy fleet-wide safety message carrying the GO FAST video.

### What it is

A January 2015 email from the operations officer of US Fleet Forces Command to all subordinate commanders, subject line 'URGENT SAFETY OF FLIGHT ISSUE,' attaching the GO FAST video and asking: 'If any of you know what these are, tell me ASAP. We are having multiple near-midair collisions, and if we do not resolve it soon, we will have to shut down the exercise.' Then-Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet, at the time Commander of Naval Meteorology and Oceanography Command, testified in writing to the House Oversight joint subcommittees on 13 November 2024 that the email disappeared from his account and from other recipients' accounts 'without explanation' the very next day, and that he concluded the information 'must have been classified within a special access program managed by an intelligence agency.' This document - or rather its deletion - is the single sharpest artefact of the concealment mechanism in the aviator record. It is not a classification decision anyone defended, not a refusal to comment, not a denial: it is the retroactive removal of an official Navy flight-safety warning from every recipient's inbox. No agency has acknowledged ordering it, and the message identifier, sender and recall authority remain unidentified.

### The board's reading

The recalled January 2015 US Fleet Forces Command message is the highest-value single artefact on the aviator strand, and it deserves separate treatment because of what it rules out. A refusal to comment is consistent with indifference. A classification decision is consistent with ordinary secrecy. But the retroactive deletion of an already-distributed fleet-wide flight-safety warning from the inboxes of every subordinate commander is not consistent with either. It requires that someone with authority over Navy messaging systems decided, within 24 hours, that a safety-of-flight warning about repeated near-midair collisions was more dangerous distributed than the near-midairs themselves. Gallaudet, a serving flag officer at the time and later acting NOAA Administrator, drew the only available inference in sworn testimony: the information 'must have been classified within a special access program managed by an intelligence agency.' The message is also the connective tissue between the two halves of the aviator record. It carried GO FAST - one of the three videos DoD later released as authentic and unidentified - and it described exactly the problem Ryan Graves's squadron was filing unacknowledged safety reports about in the same airspace in the same period. Nine years later, AARO reanalysed GO FAST on parallax grounds and concluded it showed nothing anomalous. That analysis, whatever its merits, has no bearing on why the warning was withdrawn. No agency has ever acknowledged withdrawing it, and the sender, message identifier and recall authority remain unidentified.

### Why it holds its tier

## Government Accountability Office

Government Accountability Office is a institution on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates 1921-07-01; axes C0 A1 D3; 5 connections.

In one line: Legislative branch audit agency; author of the 1995 Roswell records search.

### What it is

GAO is the audit arm of Congress and the only investigative body on this board that answers to the legislature rather than the executive. Its single most important product in this domain is GAO/NSIAD-95-187, 'Government Records: Results of a Search for Records Concerning the 1947 Crash Near Roswell, New Mexico', issued 28 July 1995 at the request of Representative Steven Schiff of New Mexico after the Air Force declined to give him a straight answer. GAO's finding is one of the load-bearing facts of the entire field: that the outgoing messages of Roswell Army Air Field for the period March 1945 to December 1949 had been destroyed, without any destruction certificate being located to authorise it. GAO could not determine who destroyed them or when. That is not a finding that the material existed; it is a finding that the record of the period was not kept in the manner required, at exactly the installation and exactly the period at issue. GAO's involvement also demonstrates the tool Congress has never fully used since: a legislative-branch auditor with statutory access authority, directed at the classification and records-management conduct of the agencies, rather than at the phenomenon.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 858 of 1082, score 18). 5 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO/NSIAD-95-187, issued 28 July 1995 at the request of Rep. Steven Schiff, reported that Roswell Army Air Field outgoing messages for March 1945 to December 1949 had been destroyed and that no destruction certificate authorising it could be located. Source: GAO/NSIAD-95-187, 28 July 1995
- **Tier 3 stated inference.** The unauthorised destruction of a single installation's outgoing message traffic covering the exact period at issue is the strongest surviving documentary anomaly in the Roswell record, and it was found by a legislative-branch auditor rather than by any executive-branch review. Reasoning: only GAO had both the access authority and the independence to look at records management rather than at the story. Source: Inference from the provenance and findings of GAO/NSIAD-95-187

## GAO accession 130581 - Controls Over Classified Documents for a Special Access Program at Lockheed Corporation

GAO accession 130581 - Controls Over Classified Documents for a Special Access Program at Lockheed Corporation is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1986-07-24; axes C1 A1 D3; 5 connections.

In one line: GAO testimony, 24 July 1986, House Energy and Commerce, Subcommittee on Oversight and Investigations.

### What it is

On 24 July 1986 the General Accounting Office told a congressional subcommittee that a prime contractor's special-access document control had failed, and in the same statement explained that the body which would ordinarily have inspected it had been excluded from doing so since 1965. That conjunction is this board's structural argument, documented and dated. The cover page reads: 'STATEMENT OF MARTIN M FERBER, ASSOCIATE DIRECTOR, NATIONAL SECURITY AND INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS DIVISION, BEFORE THE SUBCOMMITTEE ON OVERSIGHT AND INVESTIGATIONS, HOUSE COMMITTEE ON ENERGY AND COMMERCE, ON CONTROLS OVER CLASSIFIED DOCUMENTS FOR A SPECIAL ACCESS PROGRAM AT LOCKHEED CORPORATION'. The venue is worth noting: not a defence committee.

The numbers are exact and the popular version of them is wrong. Lockheed reported 1,460 discrepancies in an inventory of about 40,000 secret and top-secret items. As of 19 July 1986 it had completed investigation on 235 of them; 224 were 'later accounted for' and 11 were unresolved -- 7 inadvertently destroyed and 4 not located. The 46 destroyed and the 17 transferred out of the company sit inside the 224 that were accounted for, not outside them. The genuinely unaccounted-for total is 11.

GAO explains the carve-out in its own words: special access contracts are 'carved out of the Defense Investigative Service's periodic inspection program', a 1965 decision, and by 1983 GAO estimated 'there probably were several thousand such contracts'. Its closing sentence is the finding: 'it appears inconsistent to us to establish a special access program because of security considerations and then afford it less document protection than normal classified programs.' The companion letter report to the Secretary of Defense, GAO/NSIAD-86-191 of 7 August 1986, goes further and names the plant the testimony would not.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 274 of 1082, score 39). 5 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A1/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO explains the term: special access contracts are 'carved out of the Defense Investigative Service's periodic inspection program', and 'In 1983, we estimated that there probably were several thousand such contracts.' Source: GAO accession 130581, statement of Martin M. Ferber, 24 July 1986
- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO states the exclusion as a standing policy decision: 'In 1965, DOD decided that special access programs, because of their especially sensitive nature and small number, would be excluded from supervision by the Defense Investigative Service.' Source: GAO accession 130581, statement of Martin M. Ferber, 24 July 1986
- **Tier 1 documented.** Lockheed reported 1,460 discrepancies: 'The current inventory of all 53 document control substations has been completed, and Lockheed has reported 1,460 discrepancies.' The inventory 'consists of about 40,000 secret and top-secret items.' Source: GAO accession 130581, statement of Martin M. Ferber, 24 July 1986
- **Tier 1 documented.** The companion letter report GAO/NSIAD-86-191, B-223830, 7 August 1986, to the Secretary of Defense, signed Frank C. Conahan, states that 'the deterioration of document accountability for the special access contract at Lockheed may not have occurred if the Defense Investigative Service had been permitted to conduct semiannual inspections of the contract, as it does with other contracts at Lockheed.' Source: GAO/NSIAD-86-191, Information Security: Need for DOD Inspections of Special Access Contracts, 7 August 1986
- **Tier 1 documented.** The companion report names 'the Lockheed/Burbank plant', which the testimony itself does not. The testimony carries a footnote explaining why: 'As part of DOD granting us access to the special access program to respond your request, we agreed not to identify the nature of the program or the specific DOD component or military service involved.' Source: GAO/NSIAD-86-191, 7 August 1986; and GAO accession 130581, footnote
- **Tier 1 documented.** The testimony closes: 'In summary, it appears inconsistent to us to establish a special access program because of security considerations and then afford it less document protection than normal classified programs.' Source: GAO accession 130581, statement of Martin M. Ferber, 24 July 1986
- **Tier 1 documented.** The unaccounted-for total is 11, not 17 or 46: 'Lockheed's reported results --which we have not yet had the opportunity to verify-- are that 224 documents were later accounted for, and 11 were unresolved ... Lockheed says 7 of the 11 unresolved documents were inadvertently destroyed and it could not locate the other 4.' The 46 destroyed and the 17 transferred out of the company are dispositions within the 224 accounted for. Source: GAO accession 130581, statement of Martin M. Ferber, 24 July 1986

## UAP Gerb - Battelle Memorial Institute investigation

UAP Gerb - Battelle Memorial Institute investigation is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2024; axes C1 A1 D2; 5 connections.

In one line: Video investigation, 18 Feb 2024.

### What it is

Gerb's first corporate investigation, published 18 February 2024, and the most primary-document-dense entry in his catalogue - which is why it is the best available test of his method. It traces Battelle Memorial Institute from the 1950s, when it worked with the US government and Air Force to build a machine index categorising UFO reports and, the investigation argues, to quell public interest; through Project Stork and the resulting Special Report No. 14; through Battelle's alleged direct study of UAP material, likely a shape-memory metallic alloy recovered at the 1947 Roswell site and now referred to as nitinol, a claim for which Jacques Vallee has assembled a supporting paper trail; to modern-day connections between Battelle and the personnel around Sean Kirkpatrick and the Pentagon's public UAP messaging. The description publishes its citations in full: CUFON's Progress Reports on Project Stork, a scan of Howard C. Cross's Project Stork material, the CIA reading room copy of Special Report No. 14, a Black Vault release of the Second Progress Report on Titanium Alloys, AEC Research and Development Reports at the University of North Texas digital library, a 1994 MUFON Ohio document, and the Defense.gov transcript of the Moultrie roundtable. It also candidly flags one missing citation: 'MISSING: UPDATE ON PROJECT STORK (HAVE PDF BUT NOT LINK)'. That admission is a good-faith marker worth recording.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 525 of 1082, score 29). 5 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A1/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented, allegation shaped wording as stored.** The federal literature on nitinol, one report of it written by Battelle itself, does not support a Battelle 1948-49 lineage for the alloy. NASA SP-5110, prepared for NASA by Battelle Memorial Institute in 1972, opens: 'Some 10 years ago, Buehler and Wiley of the U.S. Naval Ordnance Laboratory developed a series of engineering alloys that possess a unique mechanical (shape) memory. The generic name of the series of alloys is 55-Nitinol, where Nitinol stands for Nickel Titanium Naval Ordnance Laboratory.' The earliest Naval Ordnance Laboratory nitinol report cited anywhere in its reference list is NOLTR 61-75 of 3 August 1961; US Patent 3,174,851 was filed on 1 December 1961; and NASA CR-1433 of September 1969 records Battelle's own role as a subcontractor to Goodyear Aerospace which 'processed the basic Nitinol rod'. The allegation that Wright-Patterson-sponsored Battelle reports on nickel-titanium alloy from 1948-49 are missing from Air Force and Battelle archives is kept on this board as the allegation it is, and is unsupported by the federal literature opened here. Source: NASA SP-5110, 55-Nitinol: The Alloy With a Memory, Battelle Memorial Institute for NASA, 1972, chapter 1 p. 1 and references 12 and 74, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19720022818/downloads/19720022818.pdf ; NASA CR-1433, Nitinol Characterization Study, September 1969, foreword p. iii and p. 2, https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19690026989/downloads/19690026989.pdf ; US Patent 3,174,851, https://patents.google.com/patent/US3174851A/en

## UAP Gerb - Northrop Grumman & TRW investigation (Vol.2)

### What it is

Published 20 March 2026, this is the corporate-breakaway argument. It moves past whether Northrop Grumman engaged in retrieval and exploitation to the who, what, when, where and why, focusing on Northrop's 2002 acquisition of TRW. Its most consequential proposition is a funding-mechanism claim: extrapolating from David Grusch's remarks on Megyn Kelly, it asks whether Northrop Grumman used IRAD - Independent Research and Development funds, which are contractor-directed and only indirectly reimbursed - at the onset of the War on Terror to self-fund its own retrieval and exploitation programs, becoming unaccountable to its original government handler. That is the 'breakaway' thesis, and it is a specific, auditable financial allegation rather than a vague one. It further examines Stephanie O'Sullivan, former Principal Deputy Director of National Intelligence, former TRW engineer and Aerospace Corporation board member, whom Grusch has stated managed the crash retrieval issue alongside James Clapper at ODNI; Northrop and TRW's connections to Clapper, whom Grusch labelled as 'managing the crash retrieval issue'; the modern Northrop security apparatus under Terry Phillips; whether clandestine underground operations persist beneath the Northrop Tejon radar cross-section facility officially closed in 2011; and the fate of the Northrop Advanced Technology division, Northrop's answer to the Skunk Works. The O'Sullivan thread is structurally elegant: a single career running TRW engineering to ODNI's number-two position to the board of an FFRDC.

## Los Alamos Green Fireballs Conference

Los Alamos Green Fireballs Conference is a event on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1949; axes C1 A2 D2; 5 connections.

In one line: 16 February 1949, Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory, New Mexico.

### What it is

The first documented scientific conference convened by the United States government on unexplained aerial objects over its own nuclear installations. Held at Los Alamos Scientific Laboratory on 16 February 1949 and attended by Laboratory Director Norris Bradbury, Edward Teller, Frederick Reines and representatives of the FBI, the Atomic Energy Commission and the US military, it addressed the green fireballs that had been appearing over Los Alamos, Sandia, Kirtland and the surrounding weapons complex since December 1948. Lincoln LaPaz of the University of New Mexico's Institute of Meteoritics had been brought in by Kirtland intelligence officers because the objects superficially resembled meteors; his assessment was that they did not behave like them. Los Alamos National Laboratory confirms the conference took place and that its National Security Research Center holds the minutes. The conclusion recorded was that the fireballs were ultimately dismissed as a national security threat - which did not stop the Air Force establishing Project Twinkle ten months later to instrument them. Newly declassified records were publicised in July 2026. The jurisdictional fact is the durable one: this was handled inside the AEC's contractor-operated laboratory complex, not by the Air Force's public UFO project at Wright-Patterson, and the record ended up in a contractor archive rather than in federal custody.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 392 of 1082, score 33). 5 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A2/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** A conference was organised at Los Alamos on 16 February 1949 attended by Norris Bradbury, Frederick Reines and Edward Teller with FBI, AEC and US military representatives; the National Security Research Center holds the conference minutes. Source: Los Alamos National Laboratory, 18 November 2024
- **Tier 1 documented.** The conference ultimately dismissed the notion that the green fireballs were a significant national security threat. Source: Los Alamos National Laboratory, 18 November 2024
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** In December 1949 Project Twinkle, a network of green fireball observation and photographic stations, was established but never fully implemented. Source: Contemporaneous Air Force records; FBI file 62-HQ-83894 material released in the Department of War PURSUE Release 01

### Its strongest connections

The green fireball events centred on the Los Alamos and Sandia complexes and produced a conference and the subsequent Project TWINKLE instrumented observation effort. Attested by the FBI and Air Force file material released through the 2026 PURSUE tranches. Sources: Los Alamos National Laboratory - FOIA requests at the National Security Research Center, https://www.lanl.gov/media/publications/the-vault/1124-foia-requests-at-the-national-security-research-center, dated 2024-11-18.

## Grusch PPD-19 Urgent Concern Complaint to the ICIG

Grusch PPD-19 Urgent Concern Complaint to the ICIG is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2022; axes C2 A3 D3; 5 connections.

In one line: Whistleblower complaint, 25 May 2022.

### What it is

The founding document of the modern disclosure record. Filed 25 May 2022 with the Inspector General of the Intelligence Community by Irvin Charles McCullough III - the IC's first Inspector General - on behalf of David Grusch, under Presidential Policy Directive 19 and the urgent-concern statute. It followed an earlier July 2021 protected disclosure by Grusch to the DoD Inspector General. The preponderance of the complaint is classified; Grusch's unclassified characterization under oath is that it concerns 'both material acquisition and exploitation activity' and the withholding of UAP baselining data from the intelligence professionals who brief aircrews. In July 2022 the ICIG determined the complaint credible and urgent, the statutory trigger for transmission to the congressional intelligence committees. A companion reprisal complaint alleges Grusch's identity and his UAP communications with the DoD IG were disclosed outside the IG channel; the unclassified version reported in June 2023 names Avril Haines, Sean Kirkpatrick, Susan Gough, Sen. Kirsten Gillibrand and Rep. Tim Burchett in its account of how the information moved. Grusch testified that the ICIG had no smooth mechanism to route the disclosure to the Armed Services Committees despite Title 10 equities - meaning the substantive Title 10 allegations may never have reached the committees with jurisdiction over the alleged programs.

### The board's reading

Chain of custody for the complaint, as established by sworn testimony and reporting. July 2021: Grusch makes a protected disclosure of classified information to the DoD Inspector General concerning the improper withholding and concealment of UAP-related material from Congress. 25 May 2022: Irvin Charles McCullough III of Compass Rose Legal Group, the first Inspector General of the Intelligence Community, files a PPD-19 urgent-concern complaint with the ICIG on Grusch's behalf. July 2022: the ICIG determines the complaint credible and urgent, the statutory trigger for transmission to the congressional intelligence committees. A companion reprisal complaint alleges Grusch's identity and his DoD IG communications were disclosed to DoD and IC entities outside the IG's office; the unclassified version reported in June 2023 references DNI Avril Haines, AARO director Sean Kirkpatrick, DoD spokesperson Susan Gough, Sen. Kirsten Gillibrand and Rep. Tim Burchett in its account of how the information moved. Grusch's unclassified description of the underlying subject matter, given under oath to Chairman Grothman: 'Both material acquisition and exploitation activity, also baselining the UAPs but not sharing it with, you know, intelligence professionals that are actually doing step briefs to pilots.' He further testified that the preponderance of the complaint was classified, that he knows the exact locations of held material and provided them to the Inspector General and in part to the intelligence committees, and that he had the individuals with firsthand knowledge make their own protected disclosures. He also testified to a routing failure: PPD-19 and ICD 120 send such disclosures to the intelligence committees, and there was no good mechanism for the ICIG to provide his information to the House and Senate Armed Services Committees despite Title 10 equities being at play.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 322 of 1082, score 36). 5 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Filed 25 May 2022 with the ICIG by counsel Charles McCullough III; found credible and urgent in July 2022. Source: Grusch sworn testimony 26 July 2023; The Debrief 5 June 2023; unclassified reprisal complaint reported 13 June 2023
- **Tier 1 documented.** Grusch's unclassified description of the complaint's subject: 'Both material acquisition and exploitation activity, also baselining the UAPs but not sharing it with intelligence professionals that are actually doing step briefs to pilots.' Source: House Oversight hearing transcript, 26 July 2023
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The reprisal complaint alleges Grusch's identity and his DoD IG communications were disclosed to DoD and IC entities outside the IG's office. Source: Unclassified reprisal complaint reported by Liberation Times, 13 June 2023

### Its strongest connections

Grusch testified he 'became a whistleblower through a PPD-19 urgent concern filing in May 2022 with the intelligence community inspector general.' The Debrief dates the filing 25 May 2022 and identifies counsel. ## Hospital Regional de Varginha

Hospital Regional de Varginha is a institution on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates no dates recorded; axes C0 A0 D0; 5 connections.

In one line: Medical facility.

### What it is

The facility referred to in the Varginha literature as the Hospital Regional de Varginha is registered in Brazil's national health-establishment register, CNES, as the Hospital Regional do Sul de Minas, CNES code 2761041, at Rua Rui Barbosa 158, Centro, Varginha, Minas Gerais (CEP 37002-140), under municipal administration, operating continuous 24-hour service and providing SUS outpatient care. Varginha's other registered hospitals in the same register include the Hospital Bom Pastor, run by the municipal hospital foundation, and the Hospital Unimed Varginha. The hospital is on this board because it is one of the medical facilities named in the January 1996 Varginha case as a place to which an injured creature was reportedly taken and held before being moved on, in the sequence involving military and fire-brigade personnel that is recorded elsewhere on this board as ev-varginha-1996. The case is not a historical curiosity: it was carried to Capitol Hill on its thirtieth anniversary, and on 8 July 2026 a congressional demand went to the CIA and FBI for records on the Varginha and Campinas incidents - meaning that a municipal hospital in Minas Gerais is now a named location in an American records fight. What exists here as verified fact is the facility and its registry entry; the 1996 hospital-custody sequence rests on witness testimony and journalism, not on any hospital record obtained in this pass.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 948 of 1082, score 14). 5 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D0.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** CNES record 2761041 registers the HOSPITAL REGIONAL DO SUL DE MINAS at Rua Rui Barbosa 158, Centro, Varginha, MG, CEP 37002-140, under municipal administrative sphere, with continuous 24-hour attendance and SUS outpatient service. Source: Ministério da Saúde CNES open data, establishment 2761041
- **Tier 1 documented.** Other hospitals registered in Varginha in the same national register include Hospital Bom Pastor (CNES 2761092, Fundação Hospitalar do Município de Varginha) and Hospital Unimed Varginha (CNES 2761319). Source: Ministério da Saúde CNES open data, município 317070
- **Tier 3 stated inference.** No hospital admission, transfer or incident record from January 1996 was located in this pass; the custody sequence at this facility remains testimony-based and is a wave-4 target for Brazilian archival and municipal records requests. Source: Research status
- **Tier 3 stated inference.** Referenced by the board but no dossier written yet. This node exists so the graph resolves and so the gap is visible rather than silent, see the wave-3 queue. Source: Board bookkeeping
- **Tier 3 stated inference.** The identification of the 'Hospital Regional de Varginha' of the 1996 case narrative with the registered Hospital Regional do Sul de Minas is a reasoned match on name, city and the fact that it is the only regional hospital in the municipal register. Reasoning: no contemporaneous 1996 hospital document was obtained to confirm the identification. Source: Inference from the CNES register

## House Armed Services Committee

House Armed Services Committee is a institution on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates 1947; axes C1 A3 D0; 5 connections.

In one line: HASC; conference principal under Chairman Rogers.

### What it is

HASC held the House side of the NDAA conference through the stripping and is the committee whose jurisdiction the Senate's independent Review Board would most directly have displaced. The pattern of what survived tracks committee interest closely: every enacted UAP provision routes its remedy to 'the appropriate congressional committees and congressional leadership', the defence and intelligence committees, while every removed provision routed authority to an independent agency, to the Archivist acting on a board's direction, or to a court. Sec. 1687, the funding limitation that did survive, is precisely the form of leverage that keeps the armed services committees as the gatekeeper: it does not open anything to the public, it compels description to the committees.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 702 of 1082, score 23). 5 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D0.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1687(c)(1) defines 'appropriate congressional committees' as the congressional defense committees plus HPSCI and SSCI, routing the remedy through committee channels only. Source: P.L. 118-31, Sec. 1687(c)(1), GPO
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** HASC Chairman Rogers is named with Turner as leading the effort against the UAP Disclosure Act. Source: Micah Hanks, The Debrief, 27 November 2023

### Its strongest connections

- House Armed Services Committee comes in from **FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP** (legislation, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: named as an appropriate committee. The same definition at sec. 7343(a)(1) names the House Armed Services Committee among the six bodies constituting 'appropriate committees of Congress' for the section's funding limitation. Sources: Pub. L. 118-31 div. G sec. 7343 (FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act), enacted text, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22.
- House Armed Services Committee runs out to **P.L. 118-31 Sec. 1687, limitation on funds for unreported programs** (legislation, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Retained remedy routes back to the committees. Sec. 1687 does not open anything to the public; it compels description to the defence and intelligence committees and congressional leadership. Sources: P.L. 118-31, FY2024 NDAA, GPO, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22.

## Intelligence Authorization Act for FY2024 (Div. G of PL 118-31) - secs. 7341, 7343

### What it is

Division G of Public Law 118-31. Sec. 7341 moves the annual UAP report obligation onto the Director of AARO; sec. 7343 conditions all IC funding for UAP special-access or restricted-access activity on prior disclosure of the details to the intelligence, armed services and appropriations committees and congressional leadership, and bars UAP-related independent research and development from being charged as an allowable indirect contract expense unless disclosed to Congress.
