Sources: Analysis and Assessment of Gateway Process, 9 June 1983, CIA-RDP96-00788R001700210016-5, https://archive.org/details/cia-readingroom-document-cia-rdp96-00788r001700210016-5, dated 1983-06-09.

## 32 CFR Part 117, NISPOM Rule

32 CFR Part 117, NISPOM Rule is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2021-02-24; axes C2 A2 D2; 4 connections.

In one line: regulation.

### What it is

The NISPOM ceased to be a DoD manual and became a federal regulation with the codification of 32 CFR Part 117, which now carries the rules binding every cleared contractor in the United States. For this board its value is threefold. First, it supplies the operative regulatory definition of a SAP: 'SAP means any program that is established to control access and distribution and to provide protection for particularly sensitive classified information beyond that normally required for TOP SECRET, SECRET, or CONFIDENTIAL information. A SAP can be created or continued only as authorized by a senior agency official delegated such authority pursuant to E.O. 13526.' Second, it contains the carve-out at 117.6(b)(3). Third, section 117.23 is the supplement for ACCM, SAPs, SCI, Restricted Data, Formerly Restricted Data, Transclassified Foreign Nuclear Information and Naval Nuclear Propulsion Information, and its paragraph (d)(1) provides that for DoD SAP contracts 'Contractors will implement the security requirements for SAPs codified in SAP-related policy, when established by contract' - a formulation that pushes the actual security rules out of the regulation and into contract documents that are not public. Part 117 also treats SAP information as 'proscribed information' alongside TOP SECRET, COMSEC, Restricted Data and SCI, which triggers the National Interest Determination process for entities under foreign ownership, control or influence. The whole rule is issued under the authority of section 201 of Executive Order 12829.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 62 of 1082, score 73). 6 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A2/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.23 is titled 'Supplement to this rule: Security Requirements for Alternative Compensatory Control Measures (ACCM), Special Access Programs (SAPs), Sensitive Compartmented Information (SCI), Restricted Data (RD), Formerly Restricted Data (FRD), Transclassified Foreign Nuclear Information (TFNI), and Naval Nuclear Propulsion Information (NNPI).' 117.23(a)(1) verbatim: 'The contractor will comply with the security measures reflected in this section and other documents specifically referenced, when applied by the GCA or designee as part of a contract. Acceptance of the contract security measures is a prerequisite to any negotiations leading to program participation and an area accreditation (e.g., an SCI facility or SAP facility accreditation).' Source: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.23
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.23(a)(2) provides that where a CSA-created program requires measures exceeding the rule's standards, 'the CSA-imposed higher standards specifically detailed in the contract or conveyed through other applicable directives will be binding on USG and contractor participants.' Source: 32 CFR 117.23(a)(2)
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.23(d)(1): 'DoD SAP contracts. Contractors will implement the security requirements for SAPs codified in SAP-related policy, when established by contract. These documents include, but are not limited to, statutes, E.O.s, CSA directives, instructions, manuals, regulations, standards, memorandums, and other SAP security related policy documents.' Source: 32 CFR 117.23(d)(1)
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.3(b) defines ACCM as 'security measures used by USG agencies to safeguard classified intelligence or operations when normal measures are insufficient to achieve strict need-to-know controls and where SAP controls are not required.' Source: 32 CFR 117.3(b), definition of ACCM
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.3(b) defines ACCM verbatim: 'ACCM are security measures used by USG agencies to safeguard classified intelligence or operations when normal measures are insufficient to achieve strict need-to-know controls and where SAP controls are not required.' ACCM is thus a SAP-like access control that by definition sits outside the SAP reporting statutes. Source: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.3
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.3(b) defines SAP verbatim: 'SAP means any program that is established to control access and distribution and to provide protection for particularly sensitive classified information beyond that normally required for TOP SECRET, SECRET, or CONFIDENTIAL information.' Source: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.3
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.3(b) lists SAP information as 'proscribed information' together with TOP SECRET information, COMSEC information, Restricted Data and SCI. Source: 32 CFR 117.3(b), definition of proscribed information
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.3(b): 'SAP means any program that is established to control access and distribution and to provide protection for particularly sensitive classified information beyond that normally required for TOP SECRET, SECRET, or CONFIDENTIAL information. A SAP can be created or continued only as authorized by a senior agency official delegated such authority pursuant to E.O. 13526.' Source: 32 CFR 117.3(b)

## Line item 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS' / Program Element 9999999999

Line item 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS' / Program Element 9999999999 is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1970; axes C2 A3 D3; 4 connections.

In one line: The published, legally binding budget lines that state a number and nothing else.

### What it is

The most direct answer to how money reaches a programme that does not appear as a budget line is that it does appear as a budget line - a line whose title is the word 'CLASSIFIED' and whose only other content is a dollar figure. In the funding tables of the National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2026 (Public Law 119-60, division D), twenty-five separate rows are titled 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS'. Summed, they carry $95.786 billion requested and $95.907 billion authorized. The largest single one is line 077A, Other Procurement, Air Force: $26,920,092 thousand - $26.92 billion of procurement money in one Air Force appropriation account, described by one word. Immediately above it sits line 077, 'SPECIAL UPDATE PROGRAM', at $1,178,397 thousand, an equally uninformative title with a long history in the Air Force budget. Other Procurement, Air Force is the account in which the intelligence community's aerial and space reconnaissance funding has historically been carried, and this row is what defense-budget analysts call the Air Force 'pass-through': money that appears in the Air Force's total obligational authority, counts against the Air Force topline, and is not spent by or controlled by the Air Force. On the research side the concealment is even more literal: classified research, development, test and evaluation appears under Program Element number 9999999999 - an all-nines placeholder in the Department's own programme element numbering system - with $22,264,031 thousand requested for the Air Force alone at line 293A in FY2026, and further nine-nines rows in the Navy ($2,554,769 thousand at line 259A) and Special Operations ($8,276,313 thousand at line 298A). Operation and Maintenance, Defense-Wide carries $22,750,830 thousand at line 530A; Operation and Maintenance, Army carries $2,385,523 thousand at line 590A. The three-year series computed directly from the enacted funding tables runs: FY2024, twenty-two rows, $86.912 billion authorized; FY2025, twenty-four rows, $88.682 billion; FY2026, twenty-five rows, $95.907 billion. These are the visible edges of the black budget. What they fund is in the classified annex.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 112 of 1082, score 58). 4 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Classified RDT&E appears under Program Element number 9999999999: FY2026 line 293A (Air Force) $22,264,031 thousand requested, $22,385,031 thousand authorized; line 259A (Navy) $2,554,769 thousand; line 298A $8,276,313 thousand. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** Comparable totals computed from the enacted funding tables: FY2024 (P.L. 118-31) 22 rows, $86.574 billion requested / $86.912 billion authorized; FY2025 (P.L. 118-159) 24 rows, $89.566 billion / $88.682 billion; FY2026 (P.L. 119-60) 25 rows, $95.786 billion / $95.907 billion. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** FY2026 NDAA line 077A, Other Procurement, Air Force, 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS': $26,920,092 thousand requested and authorized; the adjacent line 077 'SPECIAL UPDATE PROGRAM' carries $1,178,397 thousand. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** FY2026 line 530A, Operation and Maintenance Defense-Wide, 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS': $22,750,830 thousand; line 590A, Operation and Maintenance Army: $2,385,523 thousand. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** The FY2024 equivalent of the Air Force Other Procurement classified line was line 072A at $25,456,490 thousand; the FY2025 equivalent was line 064A at $25,476,312 thousand requested and $25,048,079 thousand authorized. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** The FY2026 NDAA funding tables contain 25 line items titled 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS' totalling $95.786 billion requested and $95.907 billion authorized (computed directly from the enacted text, Division D). Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm
- **Tier 3 stated inference.** The $26.9 billion Other Procurement, Air Force classified line is the principal 'pass-through': it is carried on the Air Force's books and counted against the Air Force topline but is not the Air Force's money to spend. Reasoning: the sum vastly exceeds any plausible Air Force-controlled classified procurement requirement, the account has historically carried national reconnaissance funding, and the Air Force has repeatedly and publicly complained that its published topline overstates its actual resources by roughly this magnitude. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm

### Its strongest connections

The link reads: The Department is the account holder for every published classified line. All 25 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS' rows in the FY2026 funding tables sit in Department of Defense appropriation accounts - Army, Navy, Air Force, Space Force, Defense-Wide and Special Operations. The link reads: The one-word line item is the public face of the annex table. Section 4001 authorises obligation of amounts in the funding tables and applies to any classified annex accompanying the Act; the printed tables show only 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS' and a number, the annex shows the programme detail. Attested by the enacted text. Sources: NDAA FY2026, Secs. 4001, and Division D tables, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm, dated 2025-12-18.

The link reads: The $26.9 billion Other Procurement, Air Force classified line is the historical pass-through. FY2026 line 077A carries $26,920,092 thousand in Other Procurement, Air Force, an account long understood by budget analysts to carry national reconnaissance funding that the Air Force does not control. Inference from the size of the line, the account's history, and the Air Force's own repeated public complaints about a pass-through of this magnitude; no document confirms the recipient. Sources: NDAA FY2026 Division D funding tables, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-119publ60/html/PLAW-119publ60.htm, dated 2025-12-18.

The link reads: Published classified line items are where MIP and NIP money physically sits in the defense budget. The FY2026 funding tables carry roughly $95.9 billion in rows titled only 'CLASSIFIED PROGRAMS'; the published MIP request for FY2026 is $33.6 billion and the NIP request $81.9 billion, neither of which appears as a named line anywhere. The inference is that the classified rows are the appropriation vehicle. No document states the mapping. ## Comer-Luna letters to Rubio, Hegseth and Ratcliffe on UAP records, February 2025

Comer-Luna letters to Rubio, Hegseth and Ratcliffe on UAP records, February 2025 is a document on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates 2025; axes C0 A1 D2; 4 connections.

In one line: Task Force opening records demand.

### What it is

The Task Force's opening move. On establishing the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets on 11 February 2025, Chairman James Comer and Chairwoman Anna Paulina Luna sent letters to Secretary of State Marco Rubio, Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth and CIA Director John Ratcliffe requesting a briefing on all UAP-related records in their possession, with the stated goal of delivering transparency to the American people. The choice of recipients is itself an argument: Rubio was one of five Senate cosponsors of the Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act and had said publicly that officials with very high clearances approached the Senate Intelligence Committee alleging secret retrieval and reverse-engineering programmes; Ratcliffe had said as DNI that UAP exhibit technologies the United States does not have. The letters put the question to three officials who had already answered it, in public, from the other side of the executive-legislative line.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 974 of 1082, score 13). 4 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D2.

## Aerospace Engineering & Advanced Projects

Aerospace Engineering & Advanced Projects is a domain on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1943-06-01; axes C2 A3 D1; 4 connections.

In one line: Industrial domain - the advanced projects shops where black aircraft and their cover stories are built.

### What it is

Advanced projects organisations are the industrial layer at which secrecy and engineering are the same activity. Lockheed's Skunk Works is the documented archetype: a walled-off division with its own security, its own contracting relationship, and a demonstrated ability to design, build and fly aircraft - U-2, A-12, F-117 - that the government publicly denied existed while they were flying. Ben Rich's own memoir describes that regime from the inside. The reason this domain matters to the board is that the same organisations are the only plausible industrial home for an exploitation programme: they hold the facility clearances, the special access programme infrastructure, the machinists and metallurgists, and the independent research and development accounts through which company money can be spent without a government line item. The dual-use route is therefore not a route at all - the capability is identical, and only the input differs. Northrop Grumman, Lockheed Martin, Raytheon and Battelle Memorial Institute recur across witness accounts precisely because they are the entities that could do it. Scoring high on this domain means an entity is one of those organisations or worked at that level inside one.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 294 of 1082, score 38). 4 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D1.

- **Tier 1 documented.** Lockheed's Advanced Development Projects designed and flew the U-2, A-12/SR-71 and F-117 under security regimes in which the aircraft's existence was officially denied while operational. Source: Ben R. Rich and Leo Janos, 'Skunk Works: A Personal Memoir of My Years at Lockheed'
- **Tier 2 named person on the record, allegation shaped wording as stored.** Lockheed Martin was publicly challenged in March 2023 over allegations concerning UFO legacy programmes, in named-source reporting. Source: Christopher Sharp, Liberation Times, 11 March 2023

## Prime Contractor Custody

Prime Contractor Custody is a domain on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1947-01-01; axes C3 A3 D1; 4 connections.

In one line: Industrial domain - the private firms alleged to hold material, and the legal shelter that creates.

### What it is

Moving a programme into a prime contractor does not merely add secrecy - it changes the legal category of everything inside it. Contractor records are not agency records and so fall outside the Freedom of Information Act; contractor facilities are inspected by the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency rather than by congressional staff; and proprietary data markings restrict government-side circulation independently of classification. GAO reported in 2004 that DoD could not provide adequate assurance that its industrial security oversight was protecting classified information at contractor facilities - a finding about the mechanism's opacity from the government's own auditor. This is the structural reason the custody question and the contractor question are the same question: an object on a contractor's shelf is legally further from Congress than the same object on a government shelf, without any change in its classification. The named firms recur across sworn and named-source testimony rather than across documents, which is precisely why this domain is scored on the C axis and researched through contract data, facility clearance records and personnel movement. Scoring high here means an entity is alleged to hold material or to have managed those who do.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 244 of 1082, score 41). 4 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D1.

- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** David Grusch testified on 26 July 2023 that material and programmes had been placed with defence contractors outside congressional oversight. Source: House Oversight Committee hearing, 26 July 2023
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** In May 2026 a group of House Republicans led by Rep. Eric Burlison sent a letter targeting private UFO contractors. Source: Newsmax, 16 May 2026

## Propulsion & Field Physics

Propulsion & Field Physics is a domain on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1956-02-01; axes C1 A2 D2; 4 connections.

In one line: Technical domain - gravitic, inertial-mass and metric-engineering approaches to flight.

### What it is

The observed flight characteristics reported by aviators - hypersonic velocity without a thermal signature, instantaneous acceleration without a control surface, transmedium travel - are incompatible with reaction propulsion, which is why every serious technical treatment of the subject ends in field physics. That is not a fringe observation but a funded one: DIA's AAWSAP reference documents include commissioned studies on vacuum spacetime metric engineering, on warp drive and negative energy, and on antigravity for aerospace applications. The independent-analysis strand is equally real - Knuth, Powell and Reali published estimates of the flight characteristics of the 2004 Nimitz object in the MaxEnt 2019 proceedings, deriving accelerations and power requirements from the reported kinematics and finding them orders of magnitude beyond known aerospace capability. The documented dual-use route runs through patents: the US Navy has prosecuted and been granted patents in the names of Salvatore Cezar Pais covering high-frequency gravitational wave generators and inertial mass reduction devices, with the Naval Air Warfare Center as assignee, and those filings are public in the USPTO record regardless of what one concludes about them. Scoring high on this domain means an entity worked, funded or filed in this space.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 467 of 1082, score 31). 4 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D2.

- **Tier 1 documented.** Knuth, Powell and Reali published 'Estimating Flight Characteristics of Anomalous Unidentified Aerial Vehicles in the 2004 Nimitz Encounter' in the MaxEnt 2019 proceedings. Source: Proceedings 2019, 33(1):26, doi:10.3390/proceedings2019033026
- **Tier 1 documented.** US Patent 10,144,532 B2, 'Craft using an inertial mass reduction device', names Salvatore Cezar Pais as inventor and the US Department of the Navy as assignee; filed 28 April 2016, granted 4 December 2018. Source: US Patent 10,144,532 B2 (Google Patents record)

## DCSA as DoD Cognizant Security Office under the NISP

DCSA as DoD Cognizant Security Office under the NISP is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1993-01-06; axes C1 A2 D2; 4 connections.

In one line: agency.

### What it is

DCSA, the successor to the Defense Security Service, is designated by 32 CFR 117.6(b)(1) as the DoD Cognizant Security Office. It administers the National Industrial Security Program 'as a separate program element on behalf of DoD GCAs and those agencies with agreements with DoD for security services' and 'Provides security oversight of the NISP as the DoD CSO on behalf of DoD components and those non-DoD executive branch agencies who enter into agreements with DoD'. It issues facility clearances for defense contractors 'to include contractors performing on DoD SAPs'. It is the only recurring, external, adversarial security inspection that a cleared contractor facility receives - and it is the entity that the carve-out provision at 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) switches off by a signature of the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense. DCSA's own performance without any carve-out was found wanting by GAO in 2004: GAO-04-332 concluded that DoD 'cannot provide adequate assurances that its oversight ensures the protection of classified information' at contractor facilities. The report is doubly relevant because GAO expressly excluded SAPs from the review at the request of committee staff - so the only public audit of the industrial security inspection system deliberately did not look at the programs the board cares about.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 162 of 1082, score 48). 5 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A2/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.6(b)(1)-(3): the Director, DCSA 'Oversees and manages DCSA, which serves as the DoD CSO', 'Administers the NISP as a separate program element', and 'Provides security oversight of the NISP as the DoD CSO' - subject to relief by an approved carve-out. Source: 32 CFR 117.6(b)
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.6(b): the Director, DCSA 'Oversees and manages DCSA, which serves as the DoD CSO', 'Administers the NISP as a separate program element on behalf of DoD GCAs and those agencies with agreements with DoD for security services', and 'Provides security oversight of the NISP as the DoD CSO on behalf of DoD components and those non-DoD executive branch agencies who enter into agreements with DoD' -- followed immediately by the carve-out sentence relieving the Director of that function for DoD SAPs on SecDef/DepSecDef approval. Source: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.6
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 para 1.2.i: 'Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency (DCSA) oversight for the administration of the National Industrial Security Program will be phased out in July 2029 in accordance with the carve-out provision.' Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDM 5205.07 Vol 1, Enclosure 2, para 2.b (then DSS, now DCSA): 'Issues facility security clearances (FCLs) for defense contractors performing on all DoD classified contracts, to include contractors performing on DoD SAPs.' Facility clearance issuance is NOT carved out -- only inspection, IS authorization and CI cross-sharing are. A carved-out SAP contractor site still has a DCSA-issued FCL and therefore still exists in DCSA's facility records. Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDM 5205.07-V1 Enclosure 2 para 2.b: the Director, DSS 'Issues facility security clearances (FCLs) for defense contractors performing on all DoD classified contracts, to include contractors performing on DoD SAPs.' Source: DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1, Enclosure 2, para 2.b
- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO-04-332 (3 March 2004) is titled 'Industrial Security: DOD Cannot Provide Adequate Assurances That Its Oversight Ensures the Protection of Classified Information' and found that DSS 'has not always followed required procedures when contractors have reported security violations and possible compromises of classified information.' Source: GAO-04-332, 3 March 2004
- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO-04-332 footnote 2: 'As agreed with committee staff, our review was limited to DSS's oversight of contractor facilities' protection of Confidential, Secret, and Top Secret information as defined in Executive Order no. 12958, as amended, and did not include DSS's oversight of special access programs at contractor facilities.' Source: GAO-04-332, footnote 2
- **Tier 3 stated inference.** Because the FCL function survives a carve-out (DoDM V1 Encl 2 para 2.b) while inspection does not, DCSA's facility clearance database remains the one government-wide index that lists a carved-out SAP contractor location as a cleared facility, even when no inspector may enter it. That makes FCL records a discovery surface that the carve-out does not close. Reasoning stated. Source: https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf

A further 1 claims sit on this node on the board and are not reproduced here.

### Its strongest connections

- DCSA as DoD Cognizant Security Office under the NISP comes in from **Carve-Out Provision (DCSA relief from NISP oversight)** (program, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Switches off the only external inspector of a contractor SAP facility. DoDM 5205.07-V1 Enclosure 2 para 2.c enumerates what DSS/DCSA does 'Unless a carve-out provision is approved' - inspections, IS authorisation and CI cross-sharing. Sources: DoD Manual 5205.07 Volume 1, 18 June 2015, Change 1 12 February 2018, https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf, dated 2018-02-12; 32 CFR 117.6 (eCFR, current), https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.6, dated 2026-08-12.
- DCSA as DoD Cognizant Security Office under the NISP comes in from **Carve-Out Provision (DCSA relief from NISP oversight)** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: relieves DCSA of NISP industrial security oversight for a DoD SAP. The regulation names the office the carve-out acts on. 32 CFR 117.6(b) makes the Director, DCSA responsible for 'security oversight of the NISP as the DoD CSO', and then (b)(3) removes it: "The Director, DCSA, will be relieved of this oversight function for DoD special access programs (SAPs) when the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense approves a carve-out provision in accordance with DoDD 5205.07, 'DoD SAP Policy'." So the effect is not that oversight passes to another body but that the cognizant security office's oversight function ceases for that programme, on the written approval of one of two named officials. Sources: 32 CFR part 117 (NISPOM Rule), full text via the eCFR renderer API, read 22 August 2026; published at 85 FR 83312, 21 December 2020, https://www.ecfr.gov/api/renderer/v1/content/enhanced/current/title-32?part=117, dated 2026-08-22.
- DCSA as DoD Cognizant Security Office under the NISP comes in from **DoDM 5205.07, DoD SAP Security Manual (Volumes 1-4)** (document, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: relieves it of oversight. The carve-out's effect runs against this body: the Manual's own definition is that the provision 'relieves DSS of its NISP obligation to perform industrial security oversight'. DSS is the predecessor of DCSA, which now holds the cognizant-security-office role under the NISP. The Manual is the instrument by which the oversight body is stood down for a given programme. Sources: Archived: DoDM 5205.07 Volume 4, 'Special Access Program (SAP) Security Manual: Marking', 10 Oct 2013, Incorporating Change 2, effective 8 September 2020 (Wayback 2026-05-18), https://web.archive.org/web/20260518063205/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507-V4p.PDF, dated 2026-05-18; DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1, 'DoD SAP Security Manual: General Procedures', 18 June 2015, Incorporating Change 1, 12 Feb 2018 (Internet Archive capture, 18 Oct 2019), https://web.archive.org/web/20191018064106/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodm/520507_vol1.pdf, dated 2018-02-12.

The link reads: Designated the DoD Cognizant Security Office by regulation. 32 CFR 117.6(b)(1): the Director, DCSA 'Oversees and manages DCSA, which serves as the DoD CSO.' The link reads: GAO found DoD could not assure adequate industrial security oversight - and excluded SAPs. ## DD Form 1910 for GoFast, Gimbal and FLIR video files

DD Form 1910 for GoFast, Gimbal and FLIR video files is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2017; axes C2 A3 D3; 4 connections.

In one line: Clearance request submitted 9 August 2017, approved 24 August 2017.

### What it is

A Department of Defense Clearance Request for Public Release of Department of Defense Information, submitted by Luis D. Elizondo in his capacity as Director, National Programs Special Management Staff, OUSD(I), on 9 August 2017 and approved on 24 August 2017. It covers three .mpg files named 'GoFast', 'Gimbal' and 'FLIR'. The stated purpose is precise and, read carefully, does not authorise publication: 'Not for publication. Research and analysis ONLY and info sharing with other USG and industry partners for the purposes of developing a database to help identify, analyze, and ultimately defeat UAS threats.' The subject matter is described in terms of UAV, balloons and other unmanned aircraft systems, with no reference to unidentified phenomena. This one form carries most of the weight in the dispute over how the videos entered the public record: it proves Elizondo processed the AATIP-associated imagery from inside OUSD(I) in August 2017, and it simultaneously undercuts the claim that the government cleared the footage for public release. The Navy's Joseph Gradisher later stated that neither the cognizant Navy offices nor DOPSR had any record of a request for unrestricted release in 2017.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 189 of 1082, score 45). 4 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D3.

- **Tier 1 documented.** Submitted by Luis D. Elizondo, Director, National Programs Special Management Staff, OUSD(I), on 9 August 2017 and approved 24 August 2017, covering three .mpg files: GoFast, Gimbal and FLIR. Source: DD Form 1910 released under FOIA, published by Blue Blurry Lines, 17 August 2019
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The version originally published by George Knapp had Elizondo's name and DoD title redacted; the unredacted FOIA copy identifies him. Source: Blue Blurry Lines, 17 August 2019

## The Debrief: Intelligence Officials Say U.S. Has Retrieved Craft of Non-Human Origin

The Debrief: Intelligence Officials Say U.S. Has Retrieved Craft of Non-Human Origin is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2023; axes C2 A2 D3; 4 connections.

In one line: Kean and Blumenthal, 5 June 2023.

### What it is

The article that made Grusch public. Written by Leslie Kean and Ralph Blumenthal - the same reporting pair behind the December 2017 New York Times AATIP story - and published by The Debrief on 5 June 2023 after major national outlets passed on it. Its architecture is what made it durable: rather than resting on Grusch alone, it carries on-record corroboration from retired Army Col. Karl Nell, the Army's UAP Task Force liaison, who calls Grusch beyond reproach and affirms the eighty-year reverse-engineering arms race as fundamentally correct; from Christopher Mellon, former Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense for Intelligence, on well-placed officials sharing detailed information; and from a serving NASIC officer under the name Jonathan Grey. It documents the July 2021 DoD IG disclosure, the May 2022 ICIG filing by McCullough, the July 2022 credible-and-urgent determination, and the April 2023 DOPSR clearances. Grusch's core claims as reported: intact and partially intact vehicles of non-human origin have been retrieved for decades; the materials have unique atomic arrangements and radiological signatures; the activity is illegally withheld from Congress by nesting inside classified programs; and individuals inside those programs raised concerns about illegal contracting.

### Why it holds its tier

### The claims on the record
